title: 'The Adapter Field Guide'

part: 'Adaptation'
status: 'ready'

## The idea in one sentence

[Lecture 29](/lectures/lecture-29) asked one question, what shape is $\Delta W$, and this
guide answers it forty-six times: every method in the PEFT library's tuner folder, one
drawing each, sorted into families by shape.

## How to read this guide

Every drawing uses the same grammar, so the figures teach by contrast. Ink outlines are
frozen structure. Green is what trains. Cyan is frozen random material. Yellow marks chosen
cells, purple marks prompt vectors, and blue marks a rotation or an action. When a figure
shows a number, it was measured in Lecture 29's run on this machine; when it quotes a claim,
the source is named. Every mechanism below was checked against the library's source code
(peft 0.20.0) before its figure was drawn; the drawing shows what the code does, and where a
method's finer points go beyond what could be verified, the entry says only what could.

This is a reference chapter, built to be skimmed, stepped through figure by figure, and come
back to. Read one family when you need it. The families:

1. Add a thin product, LoRA's line.
2. A structured product.
3. A few coefficients in a fixed basis.
4. Rotations.
5. Surgery on the weight's own subspaces.
6. Cheap vectors.
7. Leave the weights alone, work on the input.
8. Routers and mixtures.

## Add a thin product

The founding family. Everything here writes $\Delta W = CR$ or a close relative, and
everything inherits the two facts Lecture 29 measured: born at zero, merges away.

**LoRA** is the reference specimen, the method the [whole of Lecture
29](/lectures/lecture-29) walks through: a thin $C$ times a wide $R$, the waist first drawn
in [Lecture 6](/lectures/lecture-06), 0.22 percent of the model in training on this
machine's run.

**AdaLoRA** notices that a fixed $r$ everywhere is a guess. It scores each direction's
importance while training and moves rank away from layers that waste it, so the same budget
ends up wide where the task needs width and starved where it does not.

  AdaLoRA. The budget is the same; its allocation is learned.
</Figure>

**DoRA** splits each output channel of the adapted weight into a direction and a length, and
trains the lengths as their own small dial. The decomposition costs one number per channel
and lets training adjust how strongly a channel speaks separately from where it points.

</g>

<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">one number per channel carries the size</text>
</g>
</svg>
  DoRA. Length and direction, decoupled, the way Lecture 13 separated size from angle.
</Figure>

**HiRA** wants a high-rank change at low-rank cost, so it multiplies where LoRA adds: the
update is $W \circ (CR)$, the frozen weight rescaled entry by entry through a low-rank
pattern. Because $W$ itself has full rank to lend, the product can move far more directions
than $r$.

<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">W times CR, entry by entry: a full-rank patch</text>

</svg>
  HiRA. Multiply the frozen map instead of adding to it, and rank stops being the ceiling.
</Figure>

**DeLoRA** puts the update on a leash: the change is kept at a controlled size, with a
learned bound $\lambda$ capping how far the adapted weights can drift from where they
started. The circle from Lecture 29's rotation figure returns, now as a budget.

  DeLoRA. However training pulls, the update ends on or inside the circle.
</Figure>

**GLoRA** refuses to pick one place to adapt. It gives the layer five slots, the weight
becoming $W + W \circ A + B$ with three more slots for the bias, and each slot can be a
low-rank pair, a single vector, a scalar, or off, so one method covers scaling, shifting,
and bias editing at whatever budget each slot deserves.

  GLoRA. One adapter, five slots, each as cheap or as rich as you configure it.
</Figure>

**GraLoRA** cuts the matrix into a $k \times k$ grid of blocks first and gives every block
its own small waist. The parameter count matches plain LoRA at the same $r$, but the update
is assembled from local pieces, so its total rank can reach $k$ times further.

<g transform="translate(263.08 78) rotate(0 63.24000000000001 7.5)" data-s="2" data-note="local waists, richer update">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="var(--color-stroke-green)" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">k times the reach</text>

</svg>
  GraLoRA. Local waists; the blocks add their ranks.
</Figure>

**Lily** attacks depth. Neighboring layers share one thin factor, and a small global pool of
wide factors serves the whole model through a learned router, so the bill stops growing
linearly with the number of layers.

<Figure n="8">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 364.93 152" width="365" role="img" aria-label="Four layer boxes with dashed lines converging on one shared green C strip, and a blue router circle mixing a small pool of three green R strips. </Figure>

**PEANuT** gives the update a hidden layer. Instead of the linear $CR$, the adapter runs its
input through a thin projection, a small nonlinear stack, and a wide projection out, so the
weight update it expresses can bend in ways no single low-rank product can.

**TinyLoRA** is the family's extremist. It keeps a frozen SVD of the weight, routes a
handful of trainable numbers through fixed random projections, and steers the model with a
parameter count you can say out loud: its paper is titled "Learning to Reason in 13
Parameters."

**UniLoRA** makes the budget literal: one shared pool of numbers $\theta$ for the whole
model, and every entry of every adapter factor is a lookup into it through frozen random
wiring. Training moves the pool; the wiring never changes.

<g transform="translate(155.12 128) rotate(0 141.36 7.5)" data-s="2">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">every adapter entry is a lookup into θ</text>
</g>
</svg>
  UniLoRA. One pool, frozen wiring; the model's entire adaptation lives in a single short
  vector.
</Figure>

**VB-LoRA** does the same with structure: a bank of small vectors, shared model-wide, and
every row of every factor is a mixture of the bank's top few entries, chosen and weighted by
training. The factors never own their numbers; they borrow them.

  recipes.

The family also carries dials that change LoRA's birth rather than its shape. rsLoRA scales
the update by $\alpha/\sqrt{r}$ so large ranks stay stable. PiSSA initializes the factors
from $W$'s own principal directions; OLoRA starts them orthonormal via QR, the
factorization from [Lecture 13](/lectures/lecture-13); EVA starts from the directions the
task's data actually uses; LoftQ starts where quantization error is smallest, for QLoRA-style
training; CorDA builds its start context-first. Same shape, different first step.

## A structured product

The second family builds $\Delta W$ from small pieces with algebraic structure, so a few
numbers reach a large matrix.

**LoKr** uses the Kronecker product. Two small trained factors stamp a full-size update,
every entry of the first scaling a whole tile shaped like the second; Lecture 29 measured a
$28 \times 28$ and a $32 \times 32$ standing in for the full $896 \times 896$, 1,808 numbers
for 802,816 slots.

</Figure>

**LoHa** multiplies two thin products entry by entry, $(C_1 R_1) \circ (C_2 R_2)$. The bill
is exactly twice LoRA's, 2,162,688 against 1,081,344 in the measured table, and the reward is
rank: an entrywise product of two rank-$r$ matrices can reach rank $r^2$.

**MiSS** reshapes the weight into equal tiles and trains one small block that updates every
tile at once, part multiplied in, part added, so a single $r \times r$ block moves the whole
matrix.

  MiSS. One block, tiled across the matrix by construction.
</Figure>

**C3A** trains one row per block and lets rotation write the rest: each block of $\Delta W$
is circulant, every row a shifted copy of the first. A circulant matrix is a convolution in
disguise, which is why the method can apply it fast with Fourier transforms, a trick Part
VI will build properly when it reaches convolution.

  C3A. One row learns; the shift structure fills the block.

## A few coefficients in a fixed basis

The third family stores no matrix shape at all. Pick a basis once, train a few coordinates
in it, and let the transform build the dense update.

**FourierFT** trains chosen positions in the frequency plane, 1,000 per matrix in the
measured run, 0.0097 percent of the model, and an inverse Fourier transform turns them into
a full-size, generally full-rank $\Delta W$.

<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">ΔW</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(55.120000000000005 130) rotate(0 141.36 7.5)" data-s="2">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">train the spectrum; the matrix follows</text>
</g>
</svg>
  FourierFT. The JPEG bet, made about weight updates: sparse somewhere, if you pick the
  right somewhere.
</Figure>

**WaveFT** makes the same bet in a wavelet basis, whose coefficients carry scale and
position at once, so a coefficient can say "here, and this fine."

  WaveFT. Wavelets know where as well as how fine.
</Figure>

**SHiRA** picks the simplest basis of all, the entries themselves: a fixed sparse mask of
positions, $r(m+n)$ of them per matrix, each trained directly. No factors, no transform, and
because the scatter is spread across the whole matrix, the update is high-rank from the
start.

</Figure>

## Rotations

The orthogonal family from [Lecture 29](/lectures/lecture-29), expanded. Everything here
multiplies $W$ by an orthogonal $R$ instead of adding to it, and inherits the guarantee
[Lecture 13](/lectures/lecture-13) proved: lengths cannot change.

**OFT** keeps $R$ block-diagonal and stores only skew-symmetric entries, which the Cayley
transform turns into an orthogonal block. The rotation is an algebraic certainty; training
cannot break it even by accident.

  OFT. Rotation by construction; Lecture 29 measured its blocks at 14 by 64 per matrix.
</Figure>

**BOFT** builds the same promise the way an FFT builds a dense transform: from stages of
sparse butterfly rotations that compose into a dense one, reaching every coordinate in a few
crossing layers.

<Figure n="22">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 351.88 229" width="352" role="img" aria-label="Three columns of four nodes with blue butterfly crossings between them, neighbors swapped in stage one, across pairs in stage two. </Figure>

**HRA** composes mirrors. Each factor is a Householder reflection, the $I - 2uu^{\T}$ from
QR's engine room, and only the mirror normals train; a chain of $r$ reflections turns $W$
without ever being able to stretch it.

**RoAd** is the cheapest turn in the library: take the output coordinates two at a time and
give each pair its own 2D rotation angle and scale. Its smallest variant stores about one
number per output channel.

**PSOFT** rotates inside the waist. It freezes $C$ and $R$ from the weight's own SVD and
trains only a small $r \times r$ orthogonal transformation between them, so adaptation
happens strictly inside the principal subspace.

## Surgery on the weight's own subspaces

Three methods start by asking what $W$ already contains, using the subspace language of
[Lecture 9](/lectures/lecture-09), and operate on the answer.

**OSF** splits $W$ by its own SVD, freezes the top directions where old knowledge lives, and
trains only in the leftover subspace, so a new task cannot trample what the model already
does. It is built for learning tasks in sequence.

<Figure n="26">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 365.77 150" width="366" role="img" aria-label="W split into two bands: top directions frozen and protected, labeled old knowledge, and the rest free to train, labeled new task." style="max-width:100%;height:auto">
<defs>
</defs>

  OSF. The strong directions are locked; training lives in what is left.
</Figure>

**DEFT** is the surgeon: it learns a projection that erases a chosen slice of what $W$ says,
the projection algebra of [Lecture 11](/lectures/lecture-11), and writes new content in the
erased slice, with the whole operation equal to the identity at birth.

  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 378.92 89" width="379" role="img" aria-label="W beside a dashed blue box labeled erase a slice and a green box labeled write anew. Caption: erase a subspace, refill it, surgery, identity at birth." style="max-width:100%;height:auto">

**AdaMSS** decomposes the weight by SVD, clusters the directions into a few groups, and
gives each cluster its own small trainable update, so the adapter's structure follows the
weight's own geography.

## Cheap vectors

The family that refuses to buy matrices. Everything here trains vectors, and the measured
rows in Lecture 29's table are the smallest for exactly that reason.

**IA3** trains one number per channel and multiplies the activations by it: 141,312 numbers
on the measured model, 0.0286 percent, and no matrix anywhere.

**BEFT** is the humblest entry in the guide: it trains only additive bias vectors and leaves
every matrix alone. Move the intercepts, keep the slopes.

</Figure>

**LN tuning** trains only the normalization layers, the small gain-and-shift vectors that
sit between the big frozen blocks, retuning the thermostat while leaving the machine.

<Figure n="31">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 382.96000000000004 147.75" width="383" role="img" aria-label="A transformer block with attention and MLP frozen, and only the green LayerNorm strip below it in training. Caption: retune the thermostat, leave the machine." style="max-width:100%;height:auto">

</Figure>

**VeRA** is [Lecture 29's](/lectures/lecture-29) shared-pair trick, here in its guide form:
one frozen random pair for the whole model, two small trained vectors per layer, 27,648
numbers in the measured run.

**PVeRA** keeps VeRA's silhouette, one shared pair and two small trainable vectors per
layer, and refines how the shared pair is built and applied.

**RandLoRA** stacks several frozen random bases and trains small diagonal mixes that combine
them; summed low-rank pieces can reach full rank, at vector prices.

</g>
<g transform="translate(285.16 62) rotate(0 33.47999999999999 7.5)" data-s="2">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="var(--color-stroke-green)" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">full rank</text>
</g>
</svg>
  RandLoRA. Randomness supplies the directions; training supplies the amounts.
</Figure>

**FRoD** freezes two random patterns per matrix, one dense low-rank piece and one sparse
one, and trains only the small vectors that blend them, covering both smooth and spiky
updates from a fixed random menu.

**Trainable tokens** goes surgical on the embedding table from [Lecture
1](/lectures/lecture-01): train the rows of a few chosen tokens, touch nothing else. Teach
the model two new words for the price of two vectors.

## Leave the weights alone

The soft-prompt family from [Lecture 29](/lectures/lecture-29), completed. Nothing here
touches $W$ at all; everything edits the vectors the frozen model reads.

**Prompt tuning** prepends $k$ learned vectors to the input, 14,336 numbers in the measured
run, the smallest row of the whole table.

  Prompt tuning. The guide's smallest method: the input grows, the map never moves.
</Figure>

**Prefix tuning** walks the prompts in deeper: learned key and value vectors join the
attention of every layer, so the steering happens at depth instead of only at the entrance.

**P-tuning** adds a writer: a small MLP or LSTM produces the prompt vectors during training,
smoothing their optimization, and is thrown away once the vectors are learned.

**CPT** mixes learned prompt tokens into real context tokens and keeps their updates on a
leash, projecting each step back toward where the token began, so the prompt stays close to
language instead of drifting into arbitrary directions.

<Figure n="40">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 337.64 100" width="338" role="img" aria-label="Two purple task tokens beside two text tokens, enclosed in a dashed blue circle marking the projection bound. </g>
<g transform="translate(159.28 53) rotate(0 81.84000000000002 7.5)" data-s="2">
<text x="0" y="10.572" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="12px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">updates projected back</text>

</g>

  again.
</Figure>

**Multitask prompt tuning** learns one shared prompt for all tasks plus a thin per-task
tweak of it, multiplied in entrywise, so a new task costs a whisper instead of a full
prompt.

  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 418.92 88.5" width="419" role="img" aria-label="Three purple shared prompt vectors, a drawn ring, and a small green per-task thin pair. Caption: one prompt to rule, a whisper per task." style="max-width:100%;height:auto">

  Multitask prompt tuning. The shared prompt carries the family; each task rescales it.
</Figure>

**Adaption prompt**, LLaMA-Adapter in the literature, inserts learned prompt tokens into
only the top few layers and guards them with a gate initialized at zero, so on day one the
model is exactly the base model, the same promise LoRA makes with $C = 0$.

**CARTRIDGE** stores the prompt after the model has read it: a trained key-value cache,
kept as weights and loaded at serving time, so a whole corpus's worth of context can be
distilled once and reused forever.

<Figure n="43">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 431.1 158.5" width="431" role="img" aria-label="A frozen attention box fed by a small magazine of purple K and V vectors stored as weights. Caption: a whole corpus, distilled into a loadable cache." style="max-width:100%;height:auto">

</defs>

</path>
</g>

</g>

## Routers and mixtures

The last family manages adapters instead of inventing one.

**Poly** keeps an inventory of small LoRA-like skills shared across tasks, and each task
learns only its own mixture over them, so skills transfer and the mixing is the task.

**X-LoRA** routes between finished adapters: several trained LoRAs are frozen, and a small
router reads the hidden state and blends them token by token, a mixture of experts where the
experts are adapters.

  X-LoRA. Training built the specialists; routing picks the committee.
</Figure>

**Mixed** is the library's own closing move rather than a paper's: it lets different adapter
species run together in one model, a LoRA here, a LoHa there, an OFT beside them.

## Where this is going

Forty-six methods, and not one of them needed mathematics this course had yet to build. The
thin products are [Lecture 6's](/lectures/lecture-06) factorization wearing training
wheels; the rank arguments are [Lecture 8](/lectures/lecture-08); the rotations and mirrors
are [Lecture 13](/lectures/lecture-13); the projections and erasures are [Lecture
11](/lectures/lecture-11); the subspace splits are [Lecture 9](/lectures/lecture-09); the
input-side steering is [Lecture 1](/lectures/lecture-01). When the next batch of adapters
arrives, and it will, read the shape of $\Delta W$ first. It will land in one of these
families, and you will already know its geometry. [Lecture 29](/lectures/lecture-29) has
the running model where nine of these were measured; the run takes five seconds, and any
entry in this guide can be swapped into it by changing one config line.

<Callout type="check">
  1. Close the guide and sort from memory: LoRA, LoKr, OFT, IA3, prompt tuning, FourierFT.
     Which family does each belong to, and what does its $\Delta W$ look like?
  2. LoHa can reach rank $r^2$ where LoRA reaches $r$. HiRA can reach full rank. State what
     each pays for the upgrade, using only their figures.
  3. VeRA, UniLoRA, VB-LoRA and TinyLoRA all share one idea. Name it in one sentence, and
     name the price all four pay.
  4. OFT, BOFT, HRA and RoAd all keep $\norm{Wx}$ unchanged. Point at the figure element
     that guarantees it in each drawing.
  5. Swap one method from this guide into Lecture 29's sixty-step run by changing the
     config line, and compare the trainable count the receipt prints.
  6. Redraw Figure 1 from memory, labels included. The drawing is the test.
</Callout>
